=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an integer array nums and two integers, k and m.
Return the maximum sum of k non-overlapping subarrays of nums, where each subarray has a length of at least m.
 
Example 1:

Input: nums = [1,2,-1,3,3,4], k = 2, m = 2
Output: 13
Explanation:
The optimal choice is:

Subarray nums[3..5] with sum 3 + 3 + 4 = 10 (length is 3 >= m).
Subarray nums[0..1] with sum 1 + 2 = 3 (length is 2 >= m).

The total sum is 10 + 3 = 13.

Example 2:

Input: nums = [-10,3,-1,-2], k = 4, m = 1
Output: -10
Explanation:
The optimal choice is choosing each element as a subarray. The output is (-10) + 3 + (-1) + (-2) = -10.

 
Constraints:

1 <= nums.length <= 2000
-10^4 <= nums[i] <= 10^4
1 <= k <= floor(nums.length / m)
1 <= m <= 3

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def maxSum(self, nums: List[int], k: int, m: int) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's attempt to prove transition:
Let f[t][i] = max sum of t non-overlapping valid subarrays within nums[0:i]. For any optimal selection in prefix i:
- If it doesn't use element i-1, then value <= f[t][i-1].
- If it uses element i-1, let the last selected subarray be [s, i) (start s, end i), length i-s >= m. Previous t-1 subarrays lie within prefix s, value <= f[t-1][s]. Total <= f[t-1][s] + P[i]-P[s]. Taking max over s <= i-m. Conversely, combine optimal f[t-1][s] and subarray [s,i) to get valid selection. Thus recurrence.

from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maxSum(self, nums: List[int], k: int, m: int) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        prefix = [0]*(n+1)
        for i, x in enumerate(nums, 1):
            prefix[i] = prefix[i-1] + x
        NEG = -10**30
        dp_prev = [0]*(n+1)
        for t in range(1, k+1):
            dp_cur = [NEG]*(n+1)
            best = NEG
            for i in range(1, n+1):
                s = i - m
                if s >= 0 and dp_prev[s] != NEG:
                    val = dp_prev[s] - prefix[s]
                    if val > best:
                        best = val
                if best != NEG:
                    cand = prefix[i] + best
                    if cand > dp_cur[i-1]:
                        dp_cur[i] = cand
                    else:
                        dp_cur[i] = dp_cur[i-1]
                else:
                    dp_cur[i] = dp_cur[i-1]
            dp_prev = dp_cur
        return dp_prev[n]

from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maxSum(self, nums: List[int], k: int, m: int) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        prefix = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i in range(n):
            prefix[i + 1] = prefix[i] + nums[i]

        NEG = -10**30
        dp_prev = [0] * (n + 1)

        for t in range(1, k + 1):
            dp_cur = [NEG] * (n + 1)
            best = NEG

            for i in range(1, n + 1):
                s = i - m
                if s >= 0 and dp_prev[s] != NEG:
                    val = dp_prev[s] - prefix[s]
                    if val > best:
                        best = val

                if best != NEG:
                    cand = prefix[i] + best
                    if cand > dp_cur[i - 1]:
                        dp_cur[i] = cand
                    else:
                        dp_cur[i] = dp_cur[i - 1]
                else:
                    dp_cur[i] = dp_cur[i - 1]

            dp_prev = dp_cur

        return dp_prev[n]

For i=2,m=3, s=-1 no update; no candidate. Good.

In our DP, prefix length s means indices 0..s-1. Last subarray [s, i) means indices s..i-1. If previous interval ends at s (exclusive), it uses indices up to s-1. Last starts at s, no shared index. === ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maxSum(self, nums: List[int], k: int, m: int) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        prefix = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i in range(n):
            prefix[i + 1] = prefix[i] + nums[i]

        NEG = -10**30

        # dp_prev[i] = best sum using t-1 subarrays in nums[:i]
        dp_prev = [0] * (n + 1)

        for t in range(1, k + 1):
            dp_cur = [NEG] * (n + 1)
            best = NEG

            for i in range(1, n + 1):
                s = i - m
                if s >= 0 and dp_prev[s] != NEG:
                    best = max(best, dp_prev[s] - prefix[s])

                if best != NEG:
                    dp_cur[i] = max(dp_cur[i - 1], prefix[i] + best)
                else:
                    dp_cur[i] = dp_cur[i - 1]

            dp_prev = dp_cur

        return dp_prev[n]
```
